Condo vs. Townhouse: Which Investment Will Turn Around More Equity After a Decade?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Between 2014 and 2024, the difference in price appreciation between condos and single-family homes was less than 5 percentage points nationally, according to an analysis from Realtor.com®. Townhouses came even closer to matching the performance of detached homes—trailing by just 0.8 percentage points.

It's a remarkable outcome for the buyers opted out of the which picket fence dream, and instead invested in properties that often offer a more affordable entry point into homeownership. But that outcome may be harder to replicate in today's rapidly changing housing market.

Condo values fell 0.8% year over year in August 2026, even as single-family home values rose 1.8%, according to data from Intercontinental Exchange. And as home price growth cools, expenses that were easier to stomach during years of rapid appreciation stand to take a bigger bite out of homeowners' returns.

Even so, those national numbers tell only part of the story. In some regions, condos substantially outperformed townhouses over the decade examined by Realtor.com, challenging the notion that owning a piece of land necessarily leads to stronger appreciation.

Understanding what drove those differences—and how the economics of owning each property type are changing—offers some clues as to where buyers might find the greatest opportunity to build equity over the next decade.

Single-family homes appreciated 87.3% from 2014 to 2024, according to Realtor.com senior economist Hannah Jones. Townhomes nearly matched that pace at 86.5%, while condos trailed only slightly at 82.7%.

That modest gap are striking given the disparity in purchase prices. In October 2026, the median existing condo or co-op sold for $365,100—nearly $57,000 less than the median single-family home, according to the National Association of Realtors (NAR).

“Single-family homes are still the most popular type of homes purchased in the U.S. However, condos do appeal to buyers who want a smaller square footage and less upkeep,” explains Jessica Lautz, NAR deputy chief economist and vice president of research. “Condos are also significantly more affordable than single-family homes, which allows buyers who are downsizing or those just starting into homeownership to embrace condos.”

Still, the numbers show townhomes have been edging closer to single-family performance.

“Over the last decade, condos and townhomes appreciated almost in lockstep, but townhomes have pulled slightly ahead recently as buyers chase ‘house-like’ living without the single-family price tag,” says Jones.

While both property types have delivered strikingly similar returns from a national lens, as in all things real estate, the real gains come down to location, location, location.

In the Midwest, condo prices rose 78.3% over the last decade, outpacing the 70.7% growth of townhomes. The South followed a similar pattern, with condos climbing 66.7% compared with just 53.1% for townhomes.

The dynamic flips in higher-cost regions. In the Northeast, townhomes surged 80.5%, far ahead of the 58.6% gain for condos. And in the West, townhomes appreciated 83.8%, edging past condos at 78.9%.

Affordability explains much of the split, says Jones.

“In the more affordable Midwest and South, condos have gained ground faster than townhomes. In higher-cost regions like the Northeast and West, townhomes have appreciated more quickly,” she explains.

In lower-cost markets, condos benefit from their clustering in downtown areas and their appeal to buyers chasing city living. But in expensive, supply-constrained metros, the premium shifts toward townhomes.

“Townhomes are commanding a growing premium as buyers seek a house-like property when single-family homes are out of reach. In more affordable markets, condos—often concentrated in dense city centers—have captured stronger growth as buyers compete for limited housing options,” she adds.

“Townhomes look set for steadier growth,” says Jones. Part of that is because of what you get when you buy one.

When you purchase a townhouse, you own both the structure and the parcel of land it sits on. That land ownership gives you more control, typically fewer restrictions, and it can be a stronger driver of long-term appreciation.

“Generally townhouses have strong appreciation potential because the land itself appreciates in a more reliable way,” explains Aman Sharma, a real estate agent at SERHANT.

Eric Hughes, a real estate investor and coach with Rental Income Advisors, agrees. “The price growth of townhomes will track extremely closely with the price growth of single-family homes in general,” he says.

That connection to single-family homes is key. The “single-family feel” broadens their appeal to a wider range of buyers—families, downsizers, and even first-timers looking to get their foot in the door.

They also tend to come with lower HOA fees than condos. That not only frees up more buying power for the mortgage itself, but it also makes resale easier since future buyers are less likely to be put off by steep monthly dues or looming assessments.

Builders have taken notice, too. Townhouses accounted for a record 18.7% of single-family housing starts on a one-year moving average basis in the third quarter of 2025, according to the National Association of Home Builders.

But Jones warns that this investment isn’t free of potential headwinds. In markets where single-family inventory is expanding and prices are softening, townhome demand may take a hit as more buyers opt for detached homes instead.

“In tight urban markets, condos can still shine as buyers get creative,” says Jones.

Even though the appreciation rate of condos has lagged behind townhomes and single-family homes nationally over the last decade, they can still deliver strong returns in the right conditions.

“In big urban markets, condos are often the most desirable type of urban residence, and can appreciate strongly,” says Hughes. That means that in places like Miami Beach, New York, and San Francisco—where land is scarce and demand for city living is strong—condos dominate.

“There are certain parts of our city where highly amenitized condo high-rises perform better than townhouses from an investment standpoint,” says Miltiadis Kastanis, executive director of sales at Compass, based in Miami.

Lifestyle amenities are another draw. Concierge service, pools, gyms, and other perks make condos especially attractive to luxury buyers or second-home seekers who prioritize convenience. For full-time residents, those amenities can also translate into higher perceived value when it’s time to sell.

But again, Jones stresses that there is greater potential for some bumps in the road for condos, ranging from rising ownership costs to competition from single-family homes and townhouses.

The experts we spoke to stress that your return on investment isn’t just about appreciation and loan paydown. The hidden costs of homeownership can chip away at returns. However, it’s worth noting that even single-family homes can fall prey to these same traps.

“While single-family homes may not have an HOA fee, some communities do even for single-family homes,” says Lautz. “Maintenance can be more expensive when a homebuyer must account for costly items such as roofs, which a condo owner is unlikely to be responsible for.”

While it’s true that owners of single-family homes must shoulder all their own maintenance costs, condo owners risk getting hit with special assessments that may cover repairs or upgrades to facilities they don’t use, or work that should have been done properly the first time around.

Rick Bannon, global real estate adviser at One Sotheby’s International Realty, points to a condo in Miami where residents were hit with a $1 million special assessment just to bring the building up to safety standards.

That’s why Joe Luciano, broker at Re/Max Bentley’s, takes special care when helping his clients through a condo purchase.

“When advising clients on a condo purchase, we always want to get the meeting minutes to see if there’s any chatter around potential projects coming up and then also the financials to see if they have the appropriate amount in reserve funds to cover any surprises,” he says.

Those reserves are set to become an even more important consideration for buyers. Beginning Jan. 4, 2027, Fannie Mae will raise its standard minimum replacement reserve requirement from 10% to 15% of annual budgeted assessment income for condo projects subject to its Full Review process.

While the change could help protect condo owners against the financial fallout of deferred maintenance, it could also put upward pressure on monthly dues in underfunded communities. And buildings that fail to meet mortgage eligibility requirements could face a smaller pool of buyers.

Outside of special assessments, Luciano says monthly HOA fees can take a big bite out of a homeowner's return on investment.

Nearly 3.4 million American households paid at least $500 a month in condo or HOA fees in 2025, according to data from the U.S. Census Bureau. Roughly 1 million paid at least $1,000 a month.

“When looking at the two as investments side by side over 10 years, the appreciation could be similar, but paying an extra $300 to $500 a month is a sunk cost of $36,000 to $60,000 that didn’t go toward your mortgage principal,” explains Luciano.

At the extreme end, he points to luxury Boston high-rises with $2,000-plus monthly HOA fees that have seen stagnant appreciation in recent years.

If the last 10 years prove anything, it’s that condos and townhouses will both continue to deliver strong returns—just not always for the same reasons, or in the same places.

Townhomes have shown they can reliably shadow the trajectory of single-family homes, powered by land value and a “house-like” appeal that buyers continue to prize. Condos, by contrast, thrive where affordability and density drive demand, making them especially potent in urban cores and lower-cost regions where buyers are squeezed out of detached homes.

The next decade may sharpen these divides. As affordability pressures deepen, first-time buyers could lean more heavily into condos, reviving demand in downtowns that have seen uneven recoveries since the pandemic. At the same time, zoning reforms and the ongoing shortage of single-family homes could keep pushing buyers toward townhouses as the closest substitute for a detached home. Policy choices—from property tax incentives to how cities handle condo reserve requirements after recent safety crises—will also influence which type of property captures the bigger share of appreciation.

For investors, the lesson isn’t to pick one winner and walk away. It’s to understand how local affordability, supply constraints, and regulation interact to tilt the playing field.

Allaire Conte is a senior advice writer covering real estate and personal finance trends. She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

The marrying of the Disney+ and Hulu apps is finally coming to fruition after a years-long effort. By the end of the 2026, we'll have a unified app, according to Disney CEO Josh D'Amaro.

Disney execs said the merger is "nearly complete" in their earnings call. Although there's no exact date specified, the app unification will happen before the end of 2026. In addition to the unified Disney+ and Hulu experience, Disney also plans to introduce creator content to the platform through its recent agreement with TikTok. This is the million dollar question. Standalone subscriptions jumped from $11.99 to $12.49 with ads and from $18.99 to $21.49 without. Meanwhile, the Disney+ and Hulu Basic bundle remained unchanged at $12.99, while the Premium bundle went up just two bucks from $19.99 to $21.99 per month.

It appears that by raising the standalone costs to nearly the same price as the bundle, the strategy is to push consumers to the bundle. By nabbing this discount, you'll bypass any potential price increases that may arise over the next 12 months. And TBH, it's the best deal we'll see for a long while. AG Blanche, FBI’s Patel dodge questions about Jensen Huang investigation

📅 2026-11-24  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Attorney General Todd Blanche and FBI Director Kash Patel on Tuesday dodged questions about the conclusions of an investigation President Donald Trump cited to push Nvidia's board to bring back Jensen Huang as CEO.

Asked by a reporter whether the Department of Justice had investigated Huang and the results of that probe, Blanche said, "What the president said is that he talked to a lot of people going into the decision that he made to encourage the board to reinstate him."

"And so I'm not going to get into communications I have with the president," Blanche said at a news conference in Washington, D.C.

Huang was reinstated as CEO of Nvidia on Monday morning, less than two weeks after resigning amid allegation had had affairs with a purported spy for China and two Nvidia employees, as well as concerns that advance semiconductor chips made by his company had been diverted to China in violation of U.S. law.

In an interview with CNBC on Monday, Trump was asked what led him to personally confirm to Nvidia's board that Huang was not a national security risk.

"We've done a very thorough investigation, and the investigation showed that he did, I mean, he had bad judgment having to do with somewhat unrelated incidents, but he did nothing wrong," Trump replied.

Trump in a Truth Social post on Monday said the U.S. government had no reason to believe Huang's return to the company "poses any risk to National Security," and that Blanche had confirmed the government "does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

Nvidia, in its statement Monday announcing Huang's return, said, "The Board received input from senior levels of the United States government, and based on that input, the Board is confident that Mr. Huang's reinstatement poses no national security risk."

Blanche on Tuesday said, "Nvidia is a good corporate citizen."

"Obviously, if Nvidia was the target, or was the subject of some criminal investigation, or they were obstructing or thwarting our investigation, then that wouldn't be the message that the president is saying to the American people," Blanche said.

Patel did not answer a reporter's question of whether the FBI has spoken to Huang, and whether the FBI had been made aware of an assessment by U.S. intelligence agencies that Huang's reported paramour, Alice Huang, was an asset for China's spy agency.

Jensen Huang is not related to Alice Huang, who was an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore-based customer of Nvidia's semiconductor chips.

The DOJ and Commerce Department on Nov. 17 said they were investigating Megaspeed for potential diversion of Nvidia chips in violation of U.S. export law.

Asked how the DOJ could rule out Jensen Huang having potential criminal liability given the fact that the investigation remains ongoing, Blanche replied, "So, when you say the export investigation is still ongoing, I don't know what you're talking about."

"We have hundreds, if not more, export investigations going at any time at the Department of Justice," Blanche said.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/fewer-choices-available-enrollees-aca-exchanges-rwjf" hreflang="en">Fewer choices available to enrollees on ACA exchanges: RWJF</a>

📅 2026-11-24  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Fewer choices available to enrollees on ACA exchanges: RWJF" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The number of regions where patients have limited choices for Affordable Care Act plans is on the rise, according to a new analysis.

The Robert Wood Johnson Foundation report found that for the 2027 plan year, there are more than 200 counties in which there is only one insurer available, and close to 1,000 where there are just two plans available for consumers to choose from.

This makes for an increase of 50% from the 2026 plan year, with more than a third of counties having limited options on the ACA's marketplaces for 2027.

Most states (39) will have at least one county where plan choices are limited for enrollees. For about half of these states, this is the norm and aligns with 2026, but for the rest, this marks a significant shift, the RWJF researchers wrote in the report.

"For the individual market as a whole, the decline in participation is sobering but not a fatal blow," per the report. "The presence of federal and some state subsidies provides an important backstop that will likely prevent a complete meltdown."

"Yet the prospect of a growing number of counties with little insurer participation is grim, and likely will not bode well for premium increases, morbidity or enrollment going forward," according to the report.

The foundation cites a recent analysis from Wakely that found the individual market declined by 16% in the first six months of 2026. The end of the enhanced premium tax credits, cost pressures and growing administrative burdens are likely to contribute to worsening trends in this space.

For instance, ACA marketplace exits from Cigna, CareSource and Medica are having a significant impact in Iowa and Indiana for the coming plan year, the researchers found. In the latter case, most Indiana counties have two or fewer plan choices.

However, there are some bright spots, RWJF researchers wrote. There are certain insurers participating in the exchanges who have beefed up their footprints, and there is a smattering of new entrants. Oscar Health, for example, is planning the largest growth, entering a new state and adding new counties in some of the states it already serves.

Some insurers, like Centene and UnitedHealthcare, are making strategic exits in some markets while entering others, for a neutral impact on their footprints overall.

Insurers like Antidote Health and Peak Health are also entering new regions—even if on a limited basis in some cases—which can help staunch the bleeding.

Blue Cross Blue Shield plans also often play a key role in limited markets, as in close to 80% of counties with a single insurer, that payer is a Blues plan. In addition, in 12% of counties, the single insurer is Elevance Health, which operates plans as Anthem Blue Cross Blue Shield.

"This suggests that were it not for the somewhat unique role of Blues plans as a backstop, the threat of a bare county might be higher," per the report. "Single-state Blues, in particular, are under social and political pressure to participate in much if not all of their state, and do not feel as free to exit unprofitable counties as are publicly traded insurers."

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/academic-hospital-heads-petition-congress-2-year-delay-cms-340b-cuts" hreflang="en">Academic hospital heads petition Congress for 2-year delay to CMS' 340B cuts</a>

📅 2026-11-24  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Academic hospital heads petition Congress for 2-year delay to CMS' 340B cuts" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Dozens of academic health system and teaching hospital chief executives are turning to Congress in a bid to blunt a controversial subsidy cut coming to Medicare Part B in the new year.

Earlier this month, the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services locked in plans to reduce reimbursements for outpatient drugs dispensed by participants in the 340B Drug Pricing Program during the 2027 calendar year.

Specifically, the agency said it will move from the current rate of average sales price (ASP) plus 6% down to ASP minus 28%, with a corresponding 7.5% increase in non-drug outpatient payments to maintain budget neutrality. CMS said the change will reduce Original Medicare drug payments by an estimated $4.35 billion and drug payments from beneficiaries by $1.1 billion during the policy’s first year.

Hospital groups—particularly those that chiefly represent nonprofit or safety-net hospitals—have denounced the plan’s disproportionate effect on hospitals participating in the decades-old subsidy program, as well as a gift to for-profit hospitals that do not participate but will benefit from the budget-neutral offset.

With their formal opposition to the policy’s proposal set aside by CMS, 95 executives whose organizations are members of the Association of American Medical Colleges have now penned a letter to leaders of the House and the Senate pleading for a two-year delay to the rate cut’s implementation.

“This policy will exacerbate already negative Medicare margins at a time when teaching hospitals are already grappling with rising financial challenges resulting from inflation, workforce shortages, manufacturer-driven restrictions on the 340B program and an expected rise in uncompensated care costs,” they wrote.

The executives acknowledged that the final policy’s rate of ASP minus 28% is “slightly smaller” than CMS’ initial proposal of ASP minus 33.4%, but said the change was still a threat to the country’s healthcare safety net. They noted that the cuts represent a nearly $5.5 billion reduction that would be disproportionately shouldered by AAMC-member teaching hospitals, who comprise 17% of all impacted hospitals but will see almost $3 billion of that total.

Those funds are necessary to finance critical care services at academic hospitals including trauma care, inpatient behavioral care, transplant surgery and labor and delivery, the signatories warned lawmakers. They also noted that their institutions are major referral hubs that accept “the most complex transfer cases from lower acuity settings, such as rural and community hospitals,” in a way that for-profit hospitals and others who will benefit from the final rule do not.

“While we understand that Congress has an interest in improving the 340B program, and are broadly supportive of meaningful transparency and accountability for covered entities, we also strongly believe that cutting Medicare payment to 340B hospitals is not the appropriate policy mechanism to achieve these goals,” they wrote. “Therefore, we respectfully request that you support a two-year delay in the [Outpatient Prospective Payment System] rule’s payment cuts for 340B-acquired drugs.”

Among the letter’s 95 signatories were the heads of Cedars-Sinai, Stanford Health Care, Yale New Haven Health, Tampa General Hospital, Rush University System for Health, Mass General Brigham, Nebraska Medicine, RWJBarnabas Health, NewYork-Presbyterian, UNC Health and School of Medicine, OHSU, Jefferson Health, Penn Medicine, Vanderbilt Health and Froedtert ThedaCare.

The academic health systems and hospitals’ turn to lawmakers comes after their rebukes of CMS’ 340B policy have fallen on deaf ears. The agency and broader Trump administration have often sided with critics of the subsidy program on related initiatives like a pilot rebate program, citing 340B’s rapid growth and contribution to increased healthcare spending. A Health and Human Services Office of the Assistant Secretary for Planning and Evaluation policy brief defending the rate cut made those views crystal clear with claims that the program has incentivized provider consolidation while doing little to serve patients’ needs.

Meanwhile, Congress, spurred by public pressure to reduce healthcare costs, has proposed its own legislative fixes to 340B. Though each bill aims to preserve the program and subsidize safety-net care, some are more friendly to the hospital industry than others.

Consumer confidence shoots up after Democrats' midterm victories — though inflation worries persist

📅 2026-11-24  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Consumers grew significantly more optimistic in November in the wake of the midterm elections, with a growing number of Americans now seeing improvement in the labor market and expecting inflation to cool.

The Conference Board survey released Tuesday showed improvement across the board, with the headline confidence index jumping to 103.3, up 11.4 points from October and much better than the Dow Jones consensus forecast for 92.2.

Confidence among Democrats and independent voters improved sharply, the Conference Board said, while confidence among Republican respondents fell slightly. Still, confidence among Republicans remained the highest among the three groups.

While respondents "continued to express frustration with high prices and rising costs," they also "cited hope for the general economy," said Fergus McCormick, the board's senior economist for global indicators.

The survey comes with the Federal Reserve continuing to try to bring down inflation, which has run above the central bank's 2% target for some 5½ years. The Fed is expected to raise its benchmark interest rate by a quarter percentage point when it meets again in two weeks.

Despite the lingering worries about the cost of living — a centerpiece issue during the midterms — the survey's present situation and expectations indexes both posted strong gains. Inflation expectations for 12 months from now fell for the second straight month but remained high at 5.7%.

The survey's closely watched gap between jobs considered "plentiful" versus "hard to get" widened to 12.4 percentage points. The "plentiful" side rose to 30.8% from 26.2% while "hard to get" fell to 18.4%, down 3 percentage points.

The survey results are the second in the past week to reveal that Americans are growing more optimistic about the economy. The University of Michigan's November survey, released Friday, showed a 7.3% increase since October, with a similar partisan split.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Turning 65 in January in SoCal: Kaiser convenience vs. broader Medicare network options?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The last sentence here is very important: If one chooses Original Medicare A+B and a Medigap plan at 65 in the initial enrollment timeframe, there is no underwriting required. If one didn't like it, they could change to the Advantage plan later with no penalty. However, if they initially chose the Advantage plan and later wanted to change to Medigap after the initial allowed timeframe, then they risk failing medical underwriting. There are States where this is not so, but the OP was speaking about CA currently.
Actually, in California it is often possible to leave a Medicare Advantage and purchase a Medigap without underwriting. I learned this a few years ago from a HICAP counselor.

The relevant law is California Insurance Code 10192.12(b)(2)(D). It provides guaranteed-issue eligibility for a Medigap if the MA plan
increases premiums by 15%, increases copayments by 15%, or reduces any benefit. The "reduces any benefit" test is often met -- the counselor showed me a detailed table they put together showing nearly all MA plans in Santa Clara County met the test.

If the company issuing the MA plan also offers Medigaps, you have to buy it from them. But in the case of Kaiser, which does not offer Medigaps, you can buy from any insurer.

The counselor emphasized that it can take some arm-twisting to get the Medigap company to honor this guaranteed-issue right. Sometimes HICAP has to get their legal staff involved. I myself have not tried it so can't speak from experience.

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: When the market tanks, what do you plan to do in retirement?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

For me, the short answer is similar to some others who posted: that, bull or bear, I'll just keep on keeping on.

To respond to specific points:

(1) Roth conversion.
Never done one. Probably never will, because it would increase my gross income, and therefore increase my ACA premiums (now) or my Medicare premiums (later, due to IRMAA).
(2) Withdraw from fixed income for living expenses. (Currently withdraw from stocks for living expenses).
The asset allocation plan I'm considering for retirement is to maintain a defined allocation (maybe 50/50) between a balanced fund (e.g., VBIAX) and a short-term TIPS find (VTAPX/VTIP). Overall, this would be around the same equity percentage as Target Retirement Income (VTINX), or I might go a bit more aggressive. If I have automated withdrawals for living expenses, I'd probably set it up to take them from the TIPS fund. If a manual withdrawal is needed, I guess I'd take it from whichever one is running higher than the desired target allocation.
(3) Turn on social security. (Currently plan to take at 70 if nothing happens).
I also plan to do that at 70 (regardless of market action).
(4) Rebalance from fixed income to stocks.
Bull or bear, I'll be rebalancing in any case. The direction (toward or away from stocks) is just a matter of which gets me back to my AA.
(5) Cut spending.
I would hope my spending is appropriate with the anticipation that both bull and bear markets will come my way, so I won't have to change my spending when those same expected events happen.
(6) Tax-loss harvesting. (I don't have new investments for many years. Almost nothing to harvest).
Similar to you, I doubt I'll ever do that. My only investment in taxable with fluctuating share values is VBIAX, with no shares above $31.50 cost basis, and I think the share price won't be going back down that low. (This is why you probably won't see me in any TLH threads.)
What other things that you may suggest to do in my situation or similar situations?
As alluded above, I lean toward the usual Boglehead philosophy of not having any special reaction "when the market tanks" (other than occasional rebalancing to maintain the desired target asset allocation).

Question for you: Did you come to a decision what you plan to do?

Meet ‘Dune: Part Three’’s Ida Brooke, the Young Brit Denis Villeneuve Picked to Play Zendaya’s Daughter

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Ida Brooke is wearing a piece of Dune around her neck. The necklace is silver and unassuming, but she explains its provenance: at the end of filming Denis Villeneuve’s Dune: Part Three, silver used in the production was collected, melted down, and recast into wrap gifts for the cast. Brooke hasn’t taken hers off since.

“There’s nothing that’s going to be as good as that,” the 18-year-old actor says, turning it between her fingers over coffee in a central London café. It has changed the rest of her jewelry too: once a gold devotee, today she is wearing a stack of silver rings. (She is otherwise dressed in the Gen-Z uniform of a tank top and low-rise cargo trousers, her hair falling in glossy natural curls.) It feels particularly apt for someone whose life is currently divided into very distinct before and afters. Only a few months ago, she was a film student at the Brit School; soon, audiences will know her as Ghanima, the daughter of Zendaya’s Chani and Timothée Chalamet’s Paul Atreides, opposite Nakoa-Wolf Momoa as her twin brother, Leto II, in Villeneuve’s third Dune film.

To get there was a months-long exercise in trying not to unravel. Brooke initially self-taped without knowing she was auditioning for Dune. When her agent revealed the project, she shut the curtains on a sunny day and watched the first two films back to back. “I fell so in love with it,” she says. “I was like, ‘OK, this is going to really hurt if I don’t get this.’” By the final audition, she had already convinced herself that another girl outside the room had the advantage. Brooke went to the bathroom and gave herself a talking to. “You don’t have to get it,” she remembers thinking. “You just have to show them that you have something to say. Just make their choice a little bit harder.”

The following day, Brooke was sitting beside a teacher at school trying to solve an editing problem when a message flashed up from an unknown number: Villeneuve wanted to call her in 20 minutes. Her phone had five percent battery. She tore through the school searching for a charger, eventually hiding in a stairwell to take the call. He first warned her about the intensity of the shoot—the heat, the physical demands—and asked whether she wanted to join the film. A friend found Brooke crying and took a photograph. “She was like, ‘You might want this one day.’”

Cinema has been the organizing obsession of her life since childhood. At six, she would rewatch films endlessly on a portable DVD player; as a teenager growing up in west London, she traveled across the city for Saturday acting classes. She now lives with her father and younger sibling, a household she affectionately describes as “very rowdy.” A lifelong cinephile, though he doesn’t work in film, her father’s fascination with human psychology has informed her own approach to storytelling. (The two share a love of Martin Scorsese.) “He always loved quality stories and has really good taste,” she says, smiling.

Writing interests her as much as performing: she likes structure, psychology and—gleefully—eavesdropping, listening to “the cadence of how people speak,” she says, gesturing cheekily at the conversations trilling around us. She is currently working on a screenplay exploring Vietnam veterans and the pharmaceutical industry, a project born from the inquisitive way she approaches the world. She will also star in and executive produce an indie drama, Grass Roots, due to shoot next summer.

Calm proved instructive in the desert. Zendaya, who had already endured the elements on the earlier films, helped Brooke understand how to quiet her body enough to perform. “I feel so much more confident acting in somewhat normal environments now,” she says. Learning to act, for Brooke, has also meant learning when to pay attention and when to give in. “Denis has such a vivid imagination that you can step right into his vision… You just have to be a kid.”

Ida Brooke as Ghanima and Nakoa-Wolf Momoa as Leto in Dune: Part Three.

Brooke missed her graduation—and the screening of her final Brit School film, The Operator, which won an award—because she was away filming the forthcoming sci-fi live-action Gundam movie alongside Noah Centineo and Sydney Sweeney in Australia. When she told her new castmates how gutted she was, they improvised a graduation of sorts: everyone piled onto a sofa and screened her film together. “I had that sense of community that I missed.”

For the moment, Brooke is resisting any pressure to decide exactly what she is. Actor? Writer? Director? All three? She chose not to go to university and has instead devised her own education, meeting writers to ask how they work, reading about psychology and screenwriting, watching films, researching whatever historical period has most recently caught her imagination. “One of my biggest priorities is self-education,” she says.

Her necklace is a souvenir from one of the biggest film sets ever conceived, but Brooke looks voraciously forward at what she hasn’t encountered yet. She wants to work with Scorsese, but she also talks excitedly about young, unknown filmmakers in London: “The friends that I have now, I see them as my future colleagues.” Her ambition is not in conquering new terrain, it’s to remain porous enough to keep discovering. After all, silver can always be melted down and made into something new.

Love Blooms Behind an Open Prison’s Missing Bars in Venice and Busan-Selected Director Nidhi Saxena’s ‘Azaad Nagar’ at WAVES Film Bazaar

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Nidhi Saxena, whose films have premiered at Busan and Venice, is presenting “Azaad Nagar” (Redemption Road), a Hindi-language love story set inside an Indian open prison, at the Co-Production Market at WAVES Film Bazaar.

The film centers on Hina, a Kashmiri refugee who is convicted of murdering her husband. At the open prison, where inmates are permitted to leave during the day to work in the city but must return by 6 p.m. for roll call, she finds support and companionship in fellow prisoner Dharam. The two fall in love, but Dharam is consumed by guilt over a crime he committed 15 years earlier. His attempts at redemption include obsessively donating blood. When Hina discovers his past, she struggles to forgive him, even as she hopes the world will forgive her. Related Stories Here's How to Watch WWE Crown Jewel 2026 Live Online UFC's Event Sellout Spree Powers TKO Q3 Earnings

“In most prison films, we see stories of escape and punishment. But in this prison story, the driving force is love,” says Saxena, who co-wrote the screenplay with Ram Kumar Singh, a fellow Jaipur native familiar with the prison and its inmates.

The idea has its roots in Saxena’s childhood. An open prison in the middle of her city first introduced her to the concepts of rehabilitation and reintegration. Later, reading Dostoevsky’s “Crime and Punishment” and Victor Hugo’s “Les Misérables” deepened her interest in the relationship between crime, punishment and human change.

“I hope audiences leave asking what they would do in Hina’s place, and whether a sentence ever truly ends for the person who served it,” she says.

Saxena, an alumna of the Film and Television Institute of India, made her feature debut with “Sad Letters of an Imaginary Woman,” which premiered at the Busan International Film Festival in 2024 and received the festival’s Asian Cinema Fund post-production support. Her second feature, “Secret of a Mountain Serpent,” premiered at the Venice Film Festival in 2025.

“I didn’t even know about the concept of an open prison before. And on top of that, it was a love story between two prisoners,” says producer Naren Kumar of Mumbai-based Kyra Kumar Kreations, whose credits include co-producing the “Jolly LLB” films and producing the series “Maharani” (SonyLIV). “I was drawn to a love story that refuses easy forgiveness, to two characters asking what a person owes the world after paying for what they’ve done.”

The project is in development, with a budget of approximately INR45 million ($473,684). Around 25% of the producer’s equity is committed, with the balance being sought from co-producers and investors. The team is targeting a winter 2027 shoot and is also looking for post-production, sales and distribution partners, as well as international labs and funds.

WAVES Film Bazaar runs as the market component of the International Film Festival of India (IFFI) in Goa.

United Airlines’ Most Frequent Boeing 777-200 Flights In 2026

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Boeing 777-200 entered service in 1994 and was primarily aimed at US domestic airlines; the 777-200ER followed in 1997 with more of an international focus. The 777-200ER extends the range from around 5,240 nautical miles to 7,065 nautical miles (depending on mission profile). These are workhorses for United's transatlantic routes, as well as some inter-hub routes, routes to Hawaii, and select destinations in East Asia. The type is aging, with older examples struggling with fuel efficiency compared with modern aircraft, although as paid-off aircraft, they also offer much lower capital expenses for United.

Here is what to know about where United Airlines is flying its Boeing 777-200/ER fleet in 2026, according to data provided by Cirium, a data analytics company. The flight data below is for the full 2026 calendar year and includes both the 777-200 and the 777-200ER, as Cirium lacks distinct aircraft codes for the sub-variants. The data is also for the full year, even if the 777s were replaced with other aircraft partway through the year on the routes.

United Airlines operates a fleet of 74 Boeing 777-200/ER airliners, of which Planespotters.net lists 61 as active and 13 as parked. The overall age of the fleet is rapidly aging and is now 27.5 years old. These include 19 Boeing 777-200s, with the remainder being 777-200ERs. United is the launch customer and the largest operator of the 777-200, with the fleet being configured for domestic routes. The 777-200ER includes four examples with domestic configurations. The remainder of the 777-200ER is configured for international operations.

According to Planespotters.net, United has not divested any of its 777-200/ERs since 2005, after eight examples had been transferred to Indian and Brazilian operators. The oldest example in United's service is registered N777UA and was delivered in 1995, making it 31.5 years old. The youngest member of the family is registered as N27015 and was delivered in 2010. It is now around 26.7 years old.

Cirium's data shows United is operating the 777-200/ER from its six main hubs: San Francisco (12,591 route-pair flights), Newark (6,635), Houston (5,549), Washington Dulles (4,900), Denver (3,059), and Chicago O'Hare (1,165). Adding these directly would result in double counting of 1,759 flights due to flights between the hubs. For the full year, United is expected to operate a total of 32,140 flights with the aircraft carrying a total of 9.6 million seats.

Chicago O'Hare sees the fewest flights with United's Boeing 777-200/ERs. Its most frequent pairings (outbound and inbound) are to its other hubs, including 373 flights on the San Francisco pairing (1,604 nautical miles), 309 flights on the Los Angeles pairing (1,517 nautical miles), and 236 flights on the Denver pairing (772 nautical miles). The year is also seeing 174 flights from Chicago to Mexico's Cancun (1,255 nautical miles).

Denver is to see 3,059 United 777-200 family flights carrying 1.1 million seats. Its leading routes by distance and frequency are to Hawaii, including Honolulu (1,226 flights at 2,929 nautical miles), Maui (744 flights at 2,872 nautical miles), and Kona (238 flights at 2,894 nautical miles). That is a total of 2,248 flights on Denver's Hawaii pairings and 73% of the airports' total United 777-200/ER operations.

Other routes include Chicago O'Hare, already mentioned above (236 flights), Houston (225 flights), Cancun (174), as well as a limited number of flights to San Francisco, Orlando, and Washington Dulles. In other words, most of United's non-Hawaii paired 777-200/ER flights from Denver are to its other major hubs in the continental United States.

Examining the busy schedule of one of the most distinguished jets in the world's biggest fleet of airliners.

Washington Dulles has 4,900 United 777-200/ER operations, with the bulk of these being transatlantic flights to Western Europe. Dulles' most frequent pairing is with London Heathrow at 996 flights, followed by Germany's Frankfurt (744 flights). The next most common pairing is transpacific to Tokyo Haneda, with 728 flights. After that Japanese route, it's back to Europe with the pairings to Paris (656 flights), Lisbon (557 flights), and Rome Fiumicino (492 flights).

Other European pairings include Athens (168), Brussels (126), Barcelona (32), and Zurich (18). Domestic routes are to San Francisco (193 flights) and Los Angeles (136 flights). Most European routes from Dulles average between 3,000 and 4,000 nautical miles, although the route to Athens is considerably longer, reaching 4,492 nautical miles. United Airlines dominates Washington Dulles International Airport (IAD), accounting for roughly 66% to 70% of passenger traffic.

From Dulles, United connects the US capital with various intercontinental destinations in Europe, Asia, and even Africa. Somewhat surprisingly, given the US's strong demographic and cultural connections with South America, the only route to that continent operated by United is to São Paulo with Boeing 767-400ERs. Avianca also operates flights between Bogotá and Washington Dulles. The lack of flights is because airlines concentrate US–South America hubs in southern and coastal gateways (Miami, Houston) with stronger passenger demand.

In Houston, George Bush Intercontinental Airport (IAH) is dominated by United Airlines, while William P. Hobby Airport (HOU) is dominated by Southwest Airlines. At IAH, United boasts around 74.1% of the passenger market share, perhaps more now that Spirit has collapsed. Spirit had around 5% of the market share. From Houston, the most frequent 777-200/ER route is to London Heathrow, whose pairing is seeing 1,397 flights in 2026.

Houston is also a significant gateway to South America, with the next busiest pairing being with Buenos Aires (942 flights). The only other South American destination is Chile's Santiago, with 209 flights. These are United's main routes to South America, meaning they aren't used very heavily in that direction. There are no frequent United 777-200/ER routes to Mexico, the Caribbean, or Central America. This is partly due to many of these destinations being within range of narrowbody aircraft. For example, United operates seven daily A320-family flights between Houston and Mexico City.

The third-busiest pairing is with United's West Coast hub of San Francisco (681 flights), with another 225 flights on the Houston-Denver route and 52 flights on the Houston-Newark route. The other regular routes are transatlantic routes to Germany, with the Frankfurt pairing getting 730 flights in 2026 and Munich getting 580. Munich is the longest route at an average distance of 4,708 nautical miles, followed by Frankfurt, Buenos Aires, London Heathrow, and Santiago — all of which are above 4,000 nautical miles.

Two major US airlines currently operate the Boeing 777.

The airport that hosts the second-most United 777-200/ER flights is Newark Liberty, with 6,635 operations. United Airlines holds roughly 60% to 68% of passenger market share at Newark Liberty International Airport (EWR), making it by far the airport's dominant carrier and largest hub. It is from Newark that United connects the New York region with much of the world, including many transatlantic destinations in Europe.

Even so, the single busiest city pairing is with San Francisco, with 1,314 flights, while the second-busiest transcontinental pairing is with Los Angeles, with 710 flights. Otherwise, transatlantic routes dominate the list and include Paris (863 flights), Dublin (728 flights), Milan (582 flights), London Heathrow (413 flights), Amsterdam (284 flights), Munich (205 flights), Barcelona (184 flights), and Rome (139 flights).

Other notable city pairings include Tokyo Haneda (593 flights) and São Paulo, Brazil (526 flights). The Tokyo route is notable for being one of the longest routes United is assigning its 777-200ERs, at an average distance of 5,877 nautical miles. Currently, United is operating a daily flight to Tokyo Haneda using its 787-9s, suggesting the 777-200ERs have been pulled off the route. United is also operating 787-9s on the twin Newark-Tokyo Narita route.

San Francisco is by far the busiest hub for United's fleet of 777-200/ERs in 2026, with a total of 12,591 operations. Being located on the West Coast, San Francisco also sees the largest range of destinations, with flights to Asia, Australia, New Zealand, Europe, and domestic destinations within the United States. The busiest pairing is the one mentioned above with Newark Liberty (1,314 flights).

European routes include Frankfurt (1,077 flights), London Heathrow (744 flights), Paris (656 flights), Munich (716 flights), Rome (410 flights), Barcelona (266 flights), Zurich (262 flights), and Amsterdam (238 flights). Routes to East Asia are Tokyo Haneda (730), Beijing (728), Osaka (714), Taipei (690), Seoul (273), and Tokyo Narita (239). Already mentioned above are other hub pairings with Houston, Chicago, and Washington.

San Francisco, along with Denver, is a major hub for flights to Hawaii's Honolulu (794). San Francisco is also the only hub where United's 777-200/ERs are flying to Australia (Brisbane with 524 flights and Sydney with 296 flights) and New Zealand (Auckland with 490 flights). The Sydney route is the longest one of all for United's 777-200ERs with an average distance of 6,447 nautical miles followed by Brisbane at 6,140 nautical miles. New Zealand is a bit closer at 5,665 nautical miles.

Mason & Fifth's Belsize Park guesthouse was designed to be “domestic and low-impact”

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Photography courtesy of Mason & Fifth

Design studio Holloway Li has collaborated with architecture practice New-works to conceive the interiors of this London apartment hotel, incorporating circular features meant to stand the test of time.

Mason & Fifth guesthouses are composed of fully furnished private apartments where guests have the flexibility of staying anywhere from one night to several months at a time.

The company has three sites across London, with one in Primrose Hill, another in Westbourne Park, and now Belsize Park.

The Belsize Park location occupies Ornan Court, a 19th-century mansion block that once formed part of the Ivy Bank residential estate.

Architecture practice New-works adapted the building for hospitality use, establishing a series of communal spaces at ground level that could serve as a social hub for guests.

At its heart is a large shared kitchen, centred by a chunky tiled breakfast island, followed by a lounge area with intimate groupings of timber-frame chairs and two khaki-coloured sofas, complementing the glossy green fireplace.

Just off this space is a cosy snug that’s been painted a warm ochre hue. It’s separated from the lounge by a custom-made curtain that design studio House of Quinn made from earth-toned fabric offcuts.

Holloway Li is behind the design of the interiors. Instead of doing a through-and-through overhaul, the studio instead aimed to develop a “circular economy aesthetic that felt warm, domestic and low-impact”.

“That ambition aligned with Mason & Fifth’s own sustainability agenda and allowed us to explore how historic buildings like this can be retrofitted in imaginative and progressive ways,” said the studio’s creative director Alex Holloway.

In each of the guesthouse’s 56 apartments, the bedrooms are furnished with demountable wardrobes in lieu of fitted joinery.

The wardrobes are made up of lightweight timber parts fixed together with metal brackets rather than glue so that, if necessary, they can be unassembled, flat packed, and re-built elsewhere.

Holloway Li worked with Marble Collective to create countertops and splashbacks for the apartment kitchens; all are crafted from limestone that’s been rejected from quarries because of inconsistencies.

The top edge of the splashbacks has been left slightly craggy and unfinished, further celebrating the stone’s natural flaws.

Almost all of the walls throughout the guesthouse have been simply rendered with multi-finish plaster, and any surface imperfections have been left on show.

“In many cases, the most sustainable decision is restraint,” explained Holloway Li’s senior designer Carrie Coningsby.

“Drawing on principles akin to kintsugi and spolia, the approach celebrates intervention rather than concealing it, embedding repair into the visual language of the space,” she added, referencing historic practices focused on reuse and repair instead of replacement.

Mason & Fifth Belsize Park was shortlisted in the sustainable interior category of this year’s Dezeen Awards.

Other projects in the running included the winning Cloudhaus Hotel by RooMoo, which is outfitted with materials salvaged from abandoned mountain cabins, and the London Coffee Factory by Steve Jensen Design, which is structured around a reclaimed timber framework.

Photography courtesy of Mason & Fifth

Julius Randle has no hard feelings towards Timberwolves in return after salary-dump trade to Nets

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

MINNEAPOLIS — Monday night marked Julius Randle's first trip to Minnesota — and game against the Timberwolves — since they salary-dumped him to the Nets.

The veteran poured in 29 points against his old team, but they got the last laugh as the Nets suffered a 142-116 loss.

But Randle got a standing ovation from the Target Center crowd, and another Minnesota memory.

Brooklyn Nets forward Julius Randle, left, and Minnesota Timberwolves forward Jaden McDaniels talk after a game Monday, Nov. 23, 2026, in Minneapolis. AP Photo/Matt Krohn

"I had so much fun here. My family had a lot of fun here, made a lot of great memories, played some good basketball, a lot of good people here. I was at a point in my career where it was needed. I needed to reset and it was great. It was open arms and I'm extremely happy that I got the opportunity to do that," said Randle, who insisted there was no beef or bitterness with the Timberwolves. "It's fine. You understand the business I'm in long enough, so you understand it, you get it.

"The organization was great to me. So it really wasn't any hard feelings. You move on and I'm here now trying to make the best of the environment here. So I just gotta keep competing here, just enjoying the moment, keep playing hard. … And I was going to a place I was familiar with."

Minnesota coach Chris Finch also said the split was amicable, though he admitted beforehand he knew the Nets forward would be out to punish his old team.

"[I] loved coaching Julius. We did a lot of winning here, certainly in the playoffs. He was a huge, huge contributor there," Finch said. "He came here in less than ideal situations, 48 hours before the season started, trying to figure it out on the fly.

"It was his selflessness in the middle of that first season that really ignited our run, learning that this team needed him to be a primary playmaker. … The first two rounds of the playoffs that year, he was probably one of the top-five players in the league and helped us get to the Western Conference finals. So I know exactly the mindset he'll be in. I've seen him go out against his previous teams."

Michael Porter Jr. had just 11 points on 3-for-7 shooting, and was all but invisible for long stretches.

"I'm struggling with figuring out, letting other guys fight to their strengths and also trying to find my rhythm, find my shot," Porter told The Post.

The Nets started Randle, Porter, Mikel Brown Jr., Danny Wolf and Day'Ron Sharpe, with Egor Dëmin coming off the bench.

Dëmin had 10 points on 3-for-13 shooting and struggled with physicality.

Julius Randle of the Brooklyn Nets drives to the basket against the Minnesota Timberwolves in Minneapolis, Monday, Nov. 23, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Randle wasn't the only reunion in the Nets' game at Minnesota.

Jordi Fernández was an assistant in Denver while Tim Connelly was the team president, both of them leaving in 2022. Connelly — a Fordham grad — is the Timberwolves president, though his contract runs out at the end of the season.

"Just a very positive person, a person that connects with everybody," Fernández said. "Tim's a connector. He finds not just the good, but the great about every single person, not just the players, but everybody that works for the organization. And that is very, very, very important.

"Every person who's touched or every organization that he's touched has gotten better. Extremely hard worker, very smart, all those things he has, but the way he connects with people and makes everything functional is great."

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: are medical bills paid for a [trust] beneficiary in addition to his yearly fixed distribution taxable?

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Again, refer to https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/26/1.652(b)-2# While that is for simple trusts, you will see under https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/26/1.662(b)-1 and https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/26/1.662(b)-2 that it applies to complex trusts as well.
Did you see and read this?
You may need to review https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/26/1.643%28a%29-3 paying particular attention to the examples.
Yes. 1.643(a)-3 gives the trustee discretion to include capital gains in DNI. I stated that was possible if state law or trust documents allow it in my first post. We have always agreed on this point.

However, that does not govern making distributions. 1.652(b)-2 governs distributions and requires DNI to be distributed pro-rata (or proportionally to the amount it makes up in the DNI).

You simply can't choose to distribute only capital gains and retain other income such as tax-exempt interest. It must be distributed pro-rata.

I have used margin borrowing to buy real estate before and paid it off in short time
All my cash is fully invested in my asset allocation 85:15 and my portfolio is high seven figures in size

Is it a good idea to borrow on margin to buy VTI/VXUS now especially if market drops more ?
If you can be bothered, search back to 2008 and "market timer" 's saga of using leverage to invest in stocks.

They were working on a Phd in economics from a prestigious school. Inspired by a paper written by ?Barry Nalebuff? of Yale School of Management, they pursued a leveraged investment strategy. They were very smart -- if someone was going to understand the risks and returns of such a strategy, it was them.

At the worst they were down over $1m. I don't think they were working full time at that point - I think they were working on their dissertation. So it was a scary time for them, and they learned a lot about themselves.

The psychological insights are fascinating. It's one of the great resources of this Board, market timer 's thread.

My advice to you stands. Investigate buying out of the money call options on the S&P 500 and possibly other indices. They have leverage embedded in them (the more out of the money, the more leverage, roughly speaking). And you cannot lose more than your initial stake.

The Nine Movies Written Or Produced (But Not Directed) By Quentin Tarantino, Ranked

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Quentin Tarantino is widely known for his directorial efforts that led to such cinema classics as Pulp Fiction, Kill Bill, and Once Upon a Time in Hollywood, but the acclaimed filmmaker has a number of writing and producing credits under his belt that broaden the scope of the projects he has been involved in.

Tarantino’s latest non-directorial filmmaking project comes in the form of The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth, a sequel to Once Upon a Time in Hollywood that focuses on Brad Pitt’s cool, calm, and collected former stuntman. While Tarantino did not direct the Netflix movie (he handed those duties over to Seven and Zodiac director David Fincher), he wrote the screenplay for the movie and, in turn, imbues it with many of his trademark qualities as a filmmaker.

However, it should be noted The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth does not mark the first time Tarantino has written or produced a project without also sitting in the director's chair. From martial arts epics to vampire Westerns to grindhouse gore fests, Tarantino has writing or producing credits on nine movies helmed by other directors.

Quentin Tarantino served as an executive producer on Hostel: Part II and was billed as "presenting" the film alongside writer-director Eli Rot. His involvement centered on backing and promoting Roth's project rather than writing or directing. As an executive producer and "Presenter," Tarantino lent his name to the marketing campaign to help draw genre fans and boost visibility. As a longtime friend, mentor, and collaborator of Roth's, Tarantino helped champion the Hostel franchise and draw on grindhouse/70s exploitation influences, but Roth wrote and directed the sequel himself.

As far as sequels to horror movies go, Hostel: Part II is far from the worst the genre has had to offer. Nonetheless, it still doesn't stack up very well to the rest of the Tarantino movies he didn't direct, largely due to the quality of the movies that follow it.

Marring Hostel: Part II from start to finish is a story that bears far too much of a resemblance to its predecessor, to such an extent that the film feels more like a remake than a sequel. The more subversive, tongue-in-cheek tonal shift gives the film somewhat of a unique identity, as does the expanded look at the Elite Hunting Club, but otherwise this is an inferior sequel in just about every department.

Quentin Tarantino served as a producer, on-set creative collaborator, and actor in Planet Terror, which was paired with his own movie, Death Proof, as part of the Grindhouse double feature. While Rodriguez wrote, directed, shot, and scored Planet Terror, actress Marley Shelton noted that Tarantino was on set frequently, offered performance notes, and occasionally adjusted lines, while still deferring to Rodriguez on his segment of the film.

Originally released in the U.S. as half of the Grindhouse double feature alongside Quentin Tarantino’s Death Proof, Planet Terror fully and delightfully embraces the aesthetics of 1970s exploitation cinema, complete with intentional film scratches, missing reels, and over-the-top practical gore.

Although heavily dependent on viewers’ proclivity for campy, gross-out B-movies due to how intentionally underdeveloped the characters and story are, Planet Terror moves at the speed of a bullet and delights with its self-aware humor, practical make-up effects, and iconic turn from Rose McGowen as Cherry Darling, a woman who has a machine gun as a leg.

Quentin Tarantino wrote the original screenplay for Natural Born Killers, although he later disowned the finished product directed by Oliver Stone and received only a "story by" credit. Tarantino wrote the initial script about serial killers Mickey and Mallory Knox and hoped to direct it himself before selling an option for $10,000 to producers Jane Hamsher and Don Murphy. His draft took a more non-linear approach centered around the fictional tabloid TV show American Maniacs.

After the script went to Warner Bros. and Oliver Stone took over as director, however, Stone, David Veloz, and Richard Rutowski heavily rewrote it, shifting the tone and structure into a chronological, visually stylized road-movie satire. Because of the extensive changes to his script, Tarantino opted for a "story by" credit rather than a full screenplay credit. He publicly criticized it, said he never watched it all the way through, and later published his original screenplay to highlight its differences from the final product.

Over time, Natural Born Killers has developed a dedicated cult following, which starkly contrasts its initial reception. Upon release, Stone’s film was derided for its graphic brutality and scathing indictment on sensationalist media, going so far as to claim the press turns murderers into celebrities.

Many retrospective viewers, however, celebrate its hyper-edited visuals, shifting film formats, and focus on celebrity crime coverage as particularly prescient in an era of the 24-hour news cycle, social media algorithms, and true crime obsession. Admittedly, the satire is heavy-handed and is rather reductionistic in overlooking deeper, more systemic causes of violence, but it inarguably offers something challenging, stirring, and thought-provoking for audiences to sit with.

Following the massive success of Crouching Tiger, Hidden Dragon, Tarantino used his clout in the industry to bring legendary kung fu movie director Yuen Woo-ping and Tsui Hark’s 1993 classic to American theaters. Tarantino served as a producer and presenter for Miramax's 2001 U.S. theatrical and home video release of Yuen Woo-ping's 1993 Hong Kong film Iron Monkey. Tarantino lobbied for and lent his name to the American rollout, introducing trailers and giving interviews to promote the movie and Yuen Woo-ping to U.S. audiences.

The martial arts film bolsters some remarkable choreography and serves as a gateway to classic Hong Kong cinema to many, although the extensive alterations made by Miramax and endorsed by Tarantino to suit Western audiences’ tastes mean the re-release departs from director’s Yuen Woo-ping original vision.

Quentin Tarantino served as an executive producer and "presenter" of Eli Roth's 2005 horror movie Hostel, helping to shape the story and market the movie. More specifically, Tarantino lent his name and status to help promote and position the film for a wider release. Roth brainstormed the idea for the film while swimming at Tarantino's pool. Tarantino liked the pitch and urged Roth to start writing the draft right away. Tarantino reportedly mentored Roth during the writing process, pushing for practical details and realism, such as how an untrained character would handle a gun or how someone might escape restraints.

The original Hostel is very much steeped in the era in which it was released. Fresh off the release of Saw, Eli Roth and Quentin Tarantino took advantage of the shift away from bland remakes and slashers to horror that terrified through its depiction of gruesome bodily harm. Hostel represents perhaps the most salient example of this mid-2000s shift into what many detractors have labeled “torture porn,” although such a label overlooks the craft underlying the 2005 film.

A film with far more thought behind it than it is often given credit for, Hostel exercises an unexpected degree of restraint to its violence, which allows its poignant commentary to simmer and strike more effectively.

Quentin Tarantino did not direct, write, or edit Zhang Yimou's 2002 martial arts epic Hero, but he championed its delayed 2004 U.S. theatrical release through Miramax and lent his name to its marketing as "Quentin Tarantino Presents".

After Hero opened in Asia in 2002, Miramax delayed its U.S. rollout, although Tarantino lobbied the studio to bring the movie to American theaters. According to Tarantino, Miramax had lost faith in the project and wanted to trim it down, yet he pushed back to keep the movie's runtime intact. Miramax agreed to release an English-subtitled version with Tarantino's promotional backing, attaching the "Quentin Tarantino Presents" label to help attract martial arts and arthouse audiences.

A beautiful film punctuated by Christopher Doyle’s painterly cinematography and distinct color-coded chapters, Hero further compels with its Rashomon-style unreliable-narrator structure and its deft blending of philosophical storytelling with wire-fu choreography.

Quentin Tarantino wrote the screenplay, served as an executive producer, and wrote a companion novelization for The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth, while stepping aside from directing in favor of David Fincher. Tarantino wrote the script as a 1977 sequel/spinoff to Once Upon a Time in Hollywood, shifting Cliff Booth (Brad Pitt) into a studio fixer and part-time projectionist/bartender.

The film leans heavily toward Tarantino's sensibilities, with his distinctive brand of irreverent humor, lengthy conversations, pulpy storytelling, and unapologetic excess dominating the experience. Fincher's trademark precision and carefully constructed visual language are certainly present, yet never quite receive the prominence one might expect from a collaboration between these two celebrated directors.

Fortunately, there's plenty to enjoy despite its shortcomings. It delivers some outstanding sequences, with its terrific opening, sustained tension, and spectacular closing stretch providing more than enough thrills to warrant buying a ticket. Brad Pitt effortlessly slips back into the role of Cliff Booth, bringing an appealing combination of effortless charisma, world-weary toughness, and unexpected depth to the character. Elizabeth Debicki is equally impressive as she produces a magnetic, captivating performance that frequently threatens to steal every scene she's in.

Tarantino’s sharp, pop-culture-heavy dialogue is fused with Tony Scott’s kinetic, singular directing style in the crime classic True Romance.

Quentin Tarantino wrote the screenplay for the 1993 crime romance film, which director Tony Scott brought to the screen. Tarantino wrote the script while working at the Video Archives store, drawing on comic-book romance tropes and his own sensibilities. Tarantino originally hoped to direct it but sold the script and used the money to help fund his directorial debut, Reservoir Dogs. Unlike Natural Born Killers, Tarantino has spoken positively about the finished movie and its cast.

Despite underperforming at the box office upon its initial release, the movie has received the plaudits it deserves over the years. Amidst the gritty crime violence of its story, the film depicts a genuinely sweet central love story, with Christian Slater and Patricia Arquette having earnest and infectious chemistry with one another.

The best Tarantino movie not directed by Tarantino himself has to be From Dusk Till Dawn, the Western vampire flick starring George Clooney and Tarantino himself.

Quentin Tarantino wrote the screenplay for and co-starred as criminal Richard "Richie" Gecko in From Dusk Till Dawn. He also served as an executive producer and helped bring director Robert Rodriguez onto the project, though he did not direct it himself. Special effects artist Robert Kurtzman created the original vampire-saloon concept. Around 1990, Kurtzman's studio (KNB EFX Group) paid Tarantino $1,500 to write the script, after which Tarantino shaped the script into a sharp pivot from a gritty crime thriller into a supernatural horror siege. After the success of Pulp Fiction, Tarantino pitched From Dusk Till Dawn to Miramax with Robert Rodriguez attached as director.

The first half plays like a tense crime thriller, one elevated by Tarantino’s signature penchant for cultivating tension through dialogue alone. But an abrupt second half genre shift moves the movie into vampire action-horror territory, which allows director Robert Rodriguez to lean into unapologetic gore, camp, and 1970s exploitation thrills. It’s no wonder that From Dusk Till Dawn spawned an entire franchise as the initial film is so rich in tension, subversion of expectations, and tantalizingly unexplored mythology.

With the release of The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth, Quentin Tarantino once again demonstrates that he is far more than just an accomplished director. His numerous writing and producing credits offer an insight into how Tarantino’s flair, influences, interests, and punchy dialogue have played critical roles in a slew of great movies over the past 33 years.

Eight years after the events of Once Upon a Time... in Hollywood, former stuntman Cliff Booth works as a Hollywood fixer, navigating the complexities and challenges of the studio system.

President Trump Says He Convinced Nvidia’s Board to Bring Back Jensen Huang

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Nvidia's board brought Jensen Huang back after "input from senior U.S. government officials."

By Johnny Rice – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:20PM EST

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday that he personally lobbied Nvidia's (NASDAQ: NVDA) board to bring back Jensen Huang as CEO, telling the network's Eamon Javers this morning that he had spoken with the board "directly and indirectly" in recent days and that he "convinced them" to reinstate Huang, who "did nothing wrong."

The crux of the issue seemed to be the national security concerns surrounding Huang's original departure: "They did ask for assurance on the national security threat," The President said, "And we gave them assurances at least with everything we know, and we know a lot."

The board's official announcement this morning echoed Trump's statements: "In the process of reaching its decision, the Board received input from senior levels of the United States government, and based on that input, the Board is confident that Mr. Huang's reinstatement poses no national security risk."

While the announcement led to a massive rally today -- Nvidia stock finished up 31% -- the President's involvement could have unintended consequences for shareholders.

The national security concern stems from Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, the former CEO of Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer accused of contravening U.S. export controls. Ms. Huang is herself a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, though Beijing denies the espionage allegation. The Justice Department is investigating Megaspeed, so too is Nvidia, which opened its own investigation on Nov. 8. The board said it has no reason to believe anyone had compromised company secrets or U.S. national security.

According to Trump, Attorney General Todd Blanche told him the government "does not possess evidence that [Huang] has engaged in any unlawful conduct" and that there's no reason to believe Huang poses a threat to national security.

Trump told CNBC that not only is he not a threat, but that through his work, he is an invaluable asset: "People say, is it a threat to have him? No, it's a threat to lose him."

Trump cheered his reinstatement, saying: "I'm very, very happy that the company had the courage and the board had the courage to do the right thing, and they suffered for two weeks, and the company suffered greatly."

Given the sensitive nature of all of this, the dramatic reversal of the board's decision would likely have raised eyebrows in Washington under any circumstance. But now that the President himself has claimed that his involvement was instrumental in changing the board's minds, I would expect serious scrutiny from Congress.

Jensen Huang. Image source: Getty Images.

Legislators on both sides of the aisle expressed their skepticism. Republican Senator John Cornyn said on the social media platform X, "No national security risk? Based on what?" while Democratic Representative Jim Himes said in a statement that "Congress still has questions that require answers."

If any evidence of wrongdoing surfaces and Huang is forced out once more, I think the market's reaction will be even stronger on the way down.
